In the August 1948 seesion of the Un1ted Nations Narcotics
Comm1 s1on, the renresentatives of 14 nations made unanimous reoom-
mendations for stringent narcotics control in Japan. Although it
may be argued that the Narcotice Commission of the United Nations
was not authorized to make recomendations on the Japanese peace
treaty, yet the technical competence of these men, as well as the
fact of their unanimity has carried much weight in the development
of my thinking. For the goal we all seek is to prevent Japan from
again becoming a center and source of illicit drug traffic, by
which means she might again, as 1n the past, weaken civilian resist-
ance to military, economic and political aggreesion.
SUMMARY:
1. The conventions on onium and dangerous druge should be includ-
ed 1n the list of treaties, re-affirmation of which will be asked
of Janan in the peace treaty.
2. Domestic legielation and administretion as practiced under the
occupation should be continued.
?. Since the peace treaty will probably prohibit manufacture of
armaments, and will exercise control and supervision in this field,
therefore, all manufacture and export of narcotic drugs should
likewise be prohibited so that they can not be used as a weapon of
warfare to weaken civilian resietance.
Whatever control body may be instituted to accom-
plish the prohibition of armaments should include
a section on dangerous drugs. The individuals in
this section should have technical experience in
drug control and administration, and knowledge of
and practical experience in the statistical obliga-
tions under the drug conventions to which Japan 1s
a party.

LEAGUE OF NATIONS
Traffic in Opium and Other Dangeroue Druge
THE DRUG SITUATION AND THE CONTROL OF NARCOTIC DRUGS IN JAPAN.
Note by the Drug Cont rol Service of the Socretariat.
The purpose of this note is to furnish the most easential infonmation
in regard to the control of drugs and the drug situation in Japan.
The information contained in the note is based on the Japanese drug
legislation and the annual reports on the traffic in opium and other
dangorous drugs furnished by the Government of Japan to the League of
Nations for the years up to and including 1938 and on other documents.
The statistical information is that supplied by Japan to the Permanent
Central Opium Board.
I.
Ratification of the International Drug Conventions.
Japan is a party to the following three of the four international
drug conventions:
(1) The Hague International Opium Convention of 1912 (January
10th, 1920).
(2) The Ganeva International Opium Convention of 1925 (October
10th, 1928).
(3) The Convantion for Limiting the Manufacture and Regulating the
Distribution of Narcotic Drug: of 1931 (June 3rd, 1935).
The dates in brackets correspond to the dates when the instruments
of ratification were deposited with the Secretariat of the League of
Nations.
II.
Laws and Regulations.
The following are the prineipal laws and regulations for the control
of nareotic drugs in force in Japan:
1. Law No. 27 of March 27th, 1898 (The Opium Law) as amended by Laws
No. 26 of 1917 and No. 43 of 1919.
2. Home Office Ordinance No. 4 of June 25th, 1919, amendod by Ordinance
No. 44 of Decmber 7th, 1928, with regulations for the enforcement
of the Opium Law.

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
3. The Japanese Ponal Code of l907, Chapter 14, concerning offences
relating to prepared opium.
4. Home Office Ordinance No. 17 of May 19th, 1930, as amended by
Home Office Ordinance No. 33 of November 20th, 1934, with regu-
lations for the Control of Narcotic Drugs.
5.
Home Office Ordinance No. 5 of March 17th, 1930, with rules for
the control of opium transported in vessels.
1.
Imperial 0rti nance No. 38 of Apri1 1st, 1931, with regulations
for the Advisory Opium Commission of the Home Office.
7.
Home Office Notification No. 149 of November 26th, 1934, to Local
Governors regarding the enforcement of rules concerning drug
addiction.
No information has been supplied by Japan in regard to legisla-
tion which might have been promulgated subsequent to 1938.
There is this peculiar feature of the Japanese Drug legislation
that the control of Opium (raw, medicinal opium and opium preparations)
is governed by law, whereas all other narcotic substances are governed
only by Home Office Ordinances, i.e., by administrative legislation.
The effect of this difference is seen in the severity of penalties.
Whereas under the Opium Law certain offences are liable to penalties of
imprisonment with hard labour up to a maximum of two years, the maximum
penalty provided in the Ordinance for the control of narcotic drugs is
only imprisonment with hard labour for not more than three months. The
penalties in the Penal Code for offences relating to prepared opium go
as high as seven years imprisonment with hard labour and in a special
case as high as 10 years.
III.
Short Analysis of the Control System.
The laws and regulations concerning Opium contain no definitions
of the substances to which the legislation applies and only mention
Opium and opium for medical purposes. It can be assumed that raw
and medicinal opium are under control but it is uncertain whether the
legislation also covers extracts and tinctures of opium.
The regulations for the control of narcotic drugs give in their
first article a definition of the substances included under the term
narcotic drugs. The list seems to cover all substances falling under
the 1925 and 1931 Conventions, except raw and medicinal opium (covered
by the Opium Law), and thebaine. Codeine and Dionine are under the
same control as other drugs with the exception that preparations con-
taining Codeine and Dionine are exempted from control. Preparations
made directly from raw or medicinal opium and preparations made direct
from the coca leaf are not specifically mentioned but they may be
considered as preparations of morphine and cocaine containing respective-
ly more than .2% of morphine or .1% of cocaine, and such preparations

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
are covered by the regulations. The l93l Convention requires control
of preparations containing other drugs in Group I than morphine and
cocaine irrespective of the drug content. The Japanese legislation
does not seem to cover any preparations containing .2% or less of
the drug, except preparations of heroin which are controlled irrespec-
tive of their heroin content.
There is a central authority for all matters concerning opium
and narcotic drugs, namely, the Medical Section of the Public Health
Bureau of the Minlatry of Welfare.
Raw opium and coca leaves are produced and he cultivation 1s
subject to licence. Raw opium produced must be sold to the Government
and only opium in receptacles sold and prepared by the Government may
be put on the market for sale.
The manufacture of drugs 1s subject to licence and manufacturers
are Government controlled and inspected.
The Ordinance for the regulation for the control of narcotic drugs
does not provide that wholesalers and retailers shall be in possession
of special licences but the Ordinance subjects wholesalers to certain
rules. On the other hand the annual reports refer to permits and
authorisations for the use or possession of drugs as being granted to
physicians, dentists, veterinarians, pharmacists and drug manufacturers.
Article 5 of the Opium Law states that medicinal opium may only be sold
by special dealers designated by local governors. In regard to other
narcotic drugs the statement is made that they may be sold only by
pharmacists licensed by the competent Ministry and by druggists author-
ised by local governors. All wholesale transactions except sales to a
pharmacy must be based on a document issued by the competent Police
authorities certifying that the purchaser is a dealer in medicine, a
physician, a dentist or a veterinarian, as the case may be, unless the
purchaser is known to the seller.
Imports and exports are subject to the system of import certificates
and export authorisations, but legislation does not seem to require
importers and exporters to possess special licences to engage in import
and export trade.
Drugs may be sold in retail against medical prescription only.
According to statistics, hemp drugs are not produced, imported or
consumed in Japan or in any of the Japanese territories.
Phyalcians are by law required to report to the Police authorities
any cases of drug addiction which they encounter in their practice.
Opium moking is forbidden by the Penal Code and subject to
severe penalties.

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.98)
-4 -
IV.
Authority responsible for Drug Control.
In January 1938 the Ministry of Welfare was established in Japan
and from that time all questions concerning narcotic drugs are the
competence of the Medical Section of the Public Health Bureau of the
Miniatry. Up to January 1938 the central authority had for many years
been the Sanitary Bureau of the Department of Home Affaire. The central
anthority was and presumal ly still 1s aasi sted by the Advisory Opium
Comm:esion created under the Imperial Ordinance No. 38 of Apri1 1st.1931.
The com.ssion is composed of officials of the different competent de-
partments and its task is to enquire into and deliberate upon methods of
cont ml of manufacture, of export and import, and of the illicit traffic
in Opium and drugs and other matters connected therewith in Japan proper,
China, Formosa and the Kwantung Leased Territory.
There 1s no information pointing to the existence in Japan of 
central authority for the suppression of the illicit traffic. It would
seem, however, that the Metropolitan Police Bureau in Tokio is responsible
for the enqu: rles into cases of intemational illicit traffic and for that
purpose maintains contacts with central authorities in other countries.
V.
Production of Raw Materials.
Japan produces Raw Opium and Coca Leaves although not enough for
domestic requirements. Raw opium is also produced in Korea (Tyosen)
and Coca Leaves in Formosa (Taiwan).
Indian Hemp is not produced in Japan proper or in any of her
Colonial territories.
Raw Opium: Anyone who desires to manufacture (produce) Raw Opiu
in Japan must obtain permission of the Governor of the Province. All
opium produced must be delivered to the Government annually by a date
fixed by the provincial governor. The opium is sent, together with
delivery note, to the prefectural governor, who forwards it to the
Tokio Hygienic Laboratory. The Opium Law has no detailed rules con-
cerning the supervision of opium production or concerning conditions
for the obtaining of permiasion to produce opium. The ordinances of
1931 and 1934 with regulations for the cont rol of narcotic drugs have
no application to Raw Opium which is not mentioned in the enumeration
of substances to which the law applies. Any special ccnditions
(limitation of area, reports on produce, inspection etc.,) which may
be imposed on cultivators will probably be indicated in the permit
issued by the provincial governor. However, Ordinance No. 4 of 1919
as amended by Ordinance No. 44 of 1928 states that opium producers
shall every year obtain permission from the provincial govemor in
regard to the locality and area of his poppy plantation at a date not
later than that fixed by the Governor. This ordinance is available

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
only in axtracts and it is possible that it contains additional control
measures relating to opium production.

opium production of Japan proper and Korea for the years 193s to 1939.

Upium produced ir. Japan and Korea is of fairly high morphine content.
averaging for each year between 12 and 13%. Opium 1s paid for by the
Government at a price fixed on the basis of its morphine content. The
latest known Irices were the following, according to notification of
April lst, 1938 by the Minist.ry of Welfare:
2.80 Yen per Kg. of Opium containing 1% but less than 2% morphine.
For Opium containing 2% morphine or more, 2.80 Yen is added to the
price per kg. for each additional 2% morphine content.
Coca Leaves: Production of Coca Leaves is subject to authorisation
granted by the Ministry of Welfare. Application is in the form of a
declaration sent through the provincial governor and stating:
1) Place and area of plantation.
2) Date for beginning the cultivation.
3) Estimate of the quantity of Leaves to be produced.
4) Address of the buildings used in connection with the cultivation
and for the purposes of storage.
Licensed cultivators have to report the following to the central
authorities through the local governor:

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
a) Place and area under cultivation within ten days after
cultivation was started.
b) Quantity of Coca Leaves harvested and disposed of before
the and of December, with indication of how they were disposed of.
Reports on these matters have to be sent in before the end of
January in the following year.
c) If culti vation is interrupted this must be reported
within ten days.
Coca Leaves harvested in Japan are not delivered to the
Government as is the case of Raw Opium but may be sold and
disposed of under the same rules as those which apply to narcotic
drugs.

VI.
Manufacture of Drugs.
Drugs are manufactured in Japan proper and Korea, in Formosa and in
Kwantung Leased Territory.
The annual report for 1938 states that in Japan proper, previous
pernission must be obtained from the Ministry of helfare for the manufacture
of al1 1rugs to w n te  inta onvent 1on of 1931 applies.
Ber re a licen e is prante1 the authorities take into careful
consideration the puantities of drugs needed for medical and scientific
purposes, for conversion, and for export as well as for reserve stocks,
with a view to limiting such quantities to the estimated requirements
for home censumption.
It seems, however, that a licence is not required for the manu-
facture of all drugs but that in the case of some (less important) drugs a
notification of intention to manufacture sent by a manufacturer through
the local governor is sufficient. The position in this respect is,
however, not ciear. The rules for the cont rol of narcotic drugs of 1930
as amended in 1934 still have Article 4 which refers to the manufacture
of certain drugs where no special authorisation is necessary but a state-
ment of intention to manufacture is sufficient. But the available trans-
lation of the ordinance being faulty, it is not possible to say which of
the drugs can be manufactured without authorisation. In the annual
report for 1934 it is said that all drugs to which the Limitation Conven-
tion is applicable have now been included among the drugs for the manu-
facture of which previous authorisation is necessary.
In cases where a licence is necessary the manufacturer must in
addition each year obtain permission from the Minister of Welfare as
regards the quantities of each drug which may be manufactured, kind and
quantities of raw materials to be used, and as regards the methods of
supply. ithin twenty days after the expiry of each quarter, manufac-
turers must send to the Minister of welfare a report as provided for in
Article 17 of the Limitation Convention of 193l. In other cases manu-
facturers have to send an annual report before the end of January in
the following year.
Drug manufacturers are subject to inspection and control in regard
to which the Annual Report for 1938 has the following statement: The
control of raw materials in stock possessed by each factory, the veri-
fication of the quantities of drugs produced by each factory, and the
supervision of disposal of the drugs by sale, export or otherwise, the
checking of the quarterly reports and the inspection of the books kept
at the factory are rigidly enforced in accordance with regulations.
If a manufacturer violates the drug ordinance or is guilty of any
offence or illegal act in regard to narcotic drugs or opium, the manu-
facturing licence may be cancelled or the manufacturer may be prohibited
from continuing his manufacture.

XI.
Control of Import and Export Trade.
The legislation does not seem to require that importers and exporters
shall be in possession of a special licence to trade in drugs but the
Home Office Ordinance of 1930 as amended makes such import and export

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
subject to the issuance of an import authorisation and an import certi-
ficate in the case of imports to Japan and an export authorisation in
the case of exports. The applications, which must give certain infor
mation specified in the ordinance, are addressed to the Minister of Wel-
fare through the Local Governor. An export application must be accom~
panied by the import certificate issued by the authorities of the
importing country. Import certificates and export authorisations are
1ssued by the Minister of helfare and must be produced to the competent
ustoms authorities on the occasion of each import and export. Importers
ant exporters must within :en days report each import and export to the
Mimister of hel fare through the Local tovermor.
The import and also internal trade in opium is under a kind of
government monopoly (see below under Section XII). Opium may be exported
with the permission of the Minister of Welfare.
XII.
Control of Wholesale and Retail Trade.
The only opium which may be sold, purchased, owned or possessed is
the Governunent opium in receptacles put up and sealed by the Government.
Medicinal opiun. may be sold only through dealers in medicinal opium
appointed for the purpose by the prefectural governors from among the
pharmacists and druggists in their prefectures. A licensed dealer may
sell opiun only in unbroken government receptacles. He may not sell at
a higher price than that fixed by the Government. In general retail
sales to physicians, dentists, veterinary surgeons, pharmacists or man-
ufacturers of medicines shall be based on a certificate issued in each
case by the administrative authorities.
Wholesalers in medicinal opium and pharmacists shall record all
sales and deliveries of opiwn, except delivery to patients on medical
prescription. The records must be kept for ten years. Viholesalers
shall make an annual report of purchases and sales which is to he sent
to the local governor within a month after the end of the fisca: year.
Prefectural governors have to send an annual report of their busiress
in opium to the Minister of Welfare.
Pharmacists may dispense opium only on presentation of a pre-
scription issued by a physician, a dentist, or a veterinary surgeon.
The ordinance for the control of narcotic drugs has no provision
for the licensing of wholesale dealers in narcotic drugs similar to those
in the Opiur. Law. However, the annual reports state etcl. year that only
pharmacies licensed by the Minister of Welfare or druggists specially
licensed by prefectural governors are authorised to sell narcotic drugs.
For every sale of narcotic drugs by a dealer to another dealer or to a
physician, a dentist, or a veterinary surgeon the dealer must demand a

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
-13-
certificate by the competent police authority, proving that the purchaser
is a dealer in drugs, a physician, a dentist, or a veterinary surgeon as
the case may be. But this rule does not apply if the purchaser is
personally known !, the seller.
Artiele l of the regulat on Por te  mt rol of nareot ie drags
requires every trader in medicines to keep a took and to enter there a
number of details in regard to each transaction in drugs, and this
includes the names and addresses of seller and purchaser. Article
19 deals with inspection and says that the local governor may send Health
officers or Police officers to inspect and transfer documents and
account books as well as to inspect all places where narcotic drugs are
manufactured, stored or sold. All narcotic drugs sold in Japan have to
be labeled as narcotic drugs and the label must give certain other
information specified in the regulations.
XIII.
Drug Addiction.
In 1934 the narcotic drug ordinance was amended by the introduction
of obligation on physicians to report within ten days to the local Police
authorities the names and addresses of any drug addicts who have been
examined by them. The report should state the drug used by the addict
and if the addict dies a new report shall be sent again within ten days.
Furthermore every physician who has given a medical prescription contain-
ing narcotic drugs to an addict in the course of treatment for addiction
shall within ten days report to the local Police office the name and
address of the patient, the name and quantity of drug prescribed, method
of use and the dose, the date of the prescription and the duration of
its validity. The result of this obligation to report addicts is that
known drug addicts in Japan are registered with the authorities. The
local Governors were given detailed instructions in the Home Office
Notification No. 149 of November 26th, 1934 to take every measure to
prevent the spreading of drug addiction and to supervise various organi-
sations working for that purpose. The Governors were instructed to keep
a register of drug addicts and to report before the end of February each
year to the Minister of Welfare in regard to the drug addicts on the
registers at the end of December in the preceding year. The rules for
the control of narcotic drugs do not make provision for compulsory curing
of addicts. But the notification to the local governors says inter alia
that arrangements shall be made to take into insane asylums addicts in
advanced stage of addiction and there give them treatment as far as
possible.

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.98)
-14 -
According to reports on addiction received from Govemors there
were at the end of 1935 about 2, 800 regi stered addicts, the majority of
whom were horeans. Correspondinw f1gures were at the end of 1936
1, 300, at the end of 1/37 . , 300 and at the end of 1938 3, 600. Some
information is given about the number of addicts cured in one special
institution in Tokio - the Musasino Hospital in which a special clinic
was established by the Metropolitan Police Bureau, accommodating 30
patients at one time. In 1935 out of 400 addicts under treatment, 370
were reported as having been fully cured; in 1936 731 out of 750, in
1937 522 out of 531, and in 1938 310 out of 316. The addicts are
given special treatment and generally freed from their craving for
drugs in about two weeks. Through charity organisations or other
suitable bodies, employment is found for former addicts on leaving the
clinic. They are given special protection and kept under direct super-
vision in order to prevent their relapse.
XIV.
Penalties.
Under the Opium Law, penalties are for certain offences imprison-
ment with hard labour for not more than two years or a fine not exceeding
1,0o0o Yen. This is the maximum penalty. Other offences are punishable
with imprisonment up to one year with hard labour or a fine not exceed-
ing lo0 Yen. Lesser offences are punishable by a fine only, not axceed-
ing 300 Yen. The Penal Code stipulates impri sonment with hard labour up
to three years for opium smokers. For illegal import, manufacture, sale
or possession for lucrative purposes of prepared opium the penalty is
imprisonment with hard labour for from six months to seven years.
Similar offences relating to moking paraphernalia are punishable with
imprisonment with hard labour for from three months to five years. The
penalty for possession of prepared opium or opium amoking paraphernalia
is imprisonment with hard labour up to one year.
It has already been mentioned that penalties for violation of the
regulations for the control of narcotic druga are much less severe than
those relating to opium and opium amoking. Under the narcotic drug
regulations the maximum penalty is impri sonment with hard labour for
three months. This applies to illicit manufacture, cultivation of the
Coca Leaf and import and export of drugs. For other offences the
penalty 1s the same but with an alternative fine of not more than 100
Yen. Lesser offences are punishable with fines up to lo0 Yen or police
detention.

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
-15-
xV.
Illicit Traffic.
When considering the illicit traffic problem in Japan a dis-
tinction has to be made between the purely domestic traffic and that
having an international character, which latter far transcends the
domestic traffic in extent and importance.
In regard to the international 1llicit traffic the Japanese
annual reports for 193s to 1938 do not  ve much information. The
following statement 1s taken from the Annual heports for 1937 and
1938: "The Government, maintaining close contact with the authorities
concerned at various ports, 1s doing its best to stop the practice by
black-listing habitual offenders, posting guards, organising special
police forces, keeping strict watch over those boarding or leaving
ships, and cooperating with shipping companies which have voluntarily
inaugurated systems of private detective service. Thanks to the
rigid control exercised and the above-mentioned efforts in particular,
illicit trafficking is recently on the decline." In l937 the report
adds: "Further illicit traffic on board Japanese ships on foreign
service has disappeared subsequent to the case of the Heian Maru"
(seizure of 25 lbs. of morphine on board the Heian Maru on March
31st,1937).
The Advisory Committee on Traffic in Opium and other Dangerous
Drugs has, however, been furnished by the representatives of the
U.S.A., Canada and China with abundant evidence of a peraistent large
1llicit traffic, originating in Japan or directed from Japan or carried
on by Japanese subjects in the U.S.A., Canada, China and other places
in the Far East.
A special aspect in regard to Japanese illicit traffic is the
methods adopted by Japan in China to encourage the illicit production
of opium, the clandestine manufacture of drugs and the abuse of opium
and narcotic drugs. These Japanese activities have, according to
reports furnished to the Opium Advi sory Committee, seriously affected
large numbers of the Chinese people and threaten to nullify the deter-
mined efforts made by the National Government of China to eradicate
opium production, opium amoking and drug addiction.
In 1936 the Advisory Committee noted in its report to the Council
that "the representatives of Canada and the United States furnished
conclusive evidence at this session of an orgalised traffic to these
countries, the North American continent, India and other Far Eastern
territories, of drugs originating from Japan".

obtain practicel information from a narcotic official in Japan
as to the failure of present Japanese regulations in controlling
narcotics.
. anufacturers are the only dealers in narcotics in

3. Manufacturere of narcotics containing less than .2%
torphine (morphine ampoules classified as such) merely are
required to notify the Ministry of  elfare they intend to manu-
facture such drugs. and to report yearly quantity made.
4. Wholesalers are not licensed but must receive permission
from the Ken governor. The Jentral Government does not have a
record of which firms are wholesnlers. They are not required to
submit reports of stock and sales of narcotics unless a special
order is issued.
5. hetailers operate under the same conditions as whole-
salers. The principle difference being that the wholesaler has
greater capital to buy a large stock of narcotics. In many cases
wholesalers and retailers are the sane entity. and when called
upon for a report of stocks may wi thhold a large anount classify-
ing it as retail stock or vice versa.
6. All doctors who roceive certificates of graduation from
a medical training school - there wero formerly 150 to 200 such
schools - register with the elfare Ministry and licensed to
deal in narcotics from then on, never registering again or re-
porting stocks of narcotics or transactions. A fee is set by
the Ministry of Welfare which a doctor pays when registering at
the beginning of his medical career. Dr. Yosumi did not know
the amount of this fee. During the war short courses. two yoars,
were instituted for training and registering doctors.

N O T E
Japan: non-fulfilment of obligations under the
1925 and 193l Narcotics Conventiona via-à-vis the Permanent
Central Oplum Board.
A general statement on this subject was made in the
Board's last report (document C.84.M.84.1945.XI., dated
July 17th, 1945, page 3, paragraph 5: "The Far East"):-
"Japan ceased all collaboration after the
outbreak of war and no returns have been received
in respect of its territories."
This general statement may be supplemented by the
following details:-
The information to be supplied to the Permanent
Central Opium Board under the International Conventions of
1925 and/or 1931 on Narcotics, 1n respect of the subatances
covered by these Conventions, 1s, mainly, as follows:-
(a) Quarterly Statistics - Imports and Exports.
(t) Annual Statistics - Production, manufacture,
conversion, conaumption (excluding that of codeine and
dionine), stocks, confiscations on account of illicit
import or export, manufacture and conaumption of
prepared (smoking opium, imports and exports of codeine
and dionine.
(c) Estimates, under the Convention of 1925, of the
quantities of "Raw Materials" to be 1mported for medical
and scientific purposes.
(d) Estimates, under the Convention of 1931, of
requirements in "Drugs". (These estimates are furnished
to the Permanent Central Opium Board but dealt with by
the Supervisory Body, to whom the Board tranamits them.)
Japan 1s a High Contracting Party to both the
Conventions of 1925 and 1931. In respect of Japan proper,
the last quarterly returns received by the Board refer to the
lst, 3rd and 4th quarters of 1940, and the last annual returns
to the year 1939. The last estimates to arrive relate to 1940.
With regard to Formosa, Korea, Kwantung and the Caroline
Marianne and Marshall Islands under Japanese mandate, the last
quarterly returns received cover the whole of l940, and the
annual the year l939. The last estimates to arrive relate to
1940 (Kwantung 194l) for those l1sted under (c) above, and to
1941 for those listed under (a).
/s/ E. Felkin
E. FELKIN.
Secretary,
Permanent Central Opium Board.

Note 1. Morphine: This heading refers to morphine in the following forms: (a) pure morphine;
(b) crude morphine; (c) salts of morphine: (d) preparations which contain more than 2o per cent of
morphine made direct from raw or medicinal opium: (c) preparations which contain more than o.2 per
cent of morphine made from any of the forms of morphine mentioned in (a). (b). (c) or (d): (f) solutions
and dilutions of morphine in an inert substance, liquid or solid. made from any of the forms of morphine
mentioned in (a), (b). (c) or (d), even if these solutions and dilutions contain o.2 per cent or less of morphine.
Where the figure entered in this column includes pure morphine contained in crude morphine, the
weight of such pure morphine and also the weight of the crude morphine should be indicated separately
under " Remarks ".

In this form the term " Geneva Convention " has been used to denote the Opium Convention signed
at Geneva on February r9th, 1925. and the term " Limitation Convention " to denote the Convention
for limiting the Manufacture and regulating the Distribution of Narcotic Drugs signed at Geneva on
July 13th, 1931.
The letters G and/or L placed at the head of a column or against an item indicate the Convention
in virtue of which the information is required — i.e.:
G = Information required in virtue of the Geneva Convention;
I. = Information required in virtue of the Limitation Convention.
A Government Party to only one Convention need only supply information when the letter denoting
that particular Convention appears both at the head of a column and against a corresponding item on
the left. The Board would, however, greatly appreciate it if a Party to only one Convention could see
its way to supplying the information required under the other Convention also.
GENERAL INSTRUCTIONS.
Show weight in kilogrammes and grammes; if impossible, state clearly the weight used in the table.
Fill in every column. Where there is nothing to report write the word "nil ".
If there is not sufficient space on this form, attach additional pages with a proper designation at the
head of each page.
Only net weights should be entered in the table (i.e., excluding packing material, such as cases,
bottles, tubes and other containers, wrappers, etc.).
Unless otherwise indicated, only the weight of the pure alkaloid content should be given in the case of
crude alkaloids and of salts and preparations. (See table of equivalences at the end of this form.)
REFERENCES.
Article 22, paragraphs 2, 3 and 4, of the Geneva Convention.
Article 13, paragraph 1, of the Limitation Convention.
For the definitions, see Article I of the Geneva and Limitation Conventions, and also Notes I and 2
on this form.
NOTES.
Note 1. Morphine: This heading refers to morphine in the following forms: (a) pure morphine;
(b) crude morphine; (c) salts of morphine; (d) preparations which contain more than 2o per cent of
morphine made direct from raw or medicinal opium; (e) preparations which contain more than o.2 per
cent of morphine made from any of the forms of morphine mentioned in (a), (b), (c) or (d); (f) solutions
and dilutions of morphine in an inert substance, liquid or solid, made from any of the forms of morphine
mentioned in (a), (b), (c) or (d), even if these solutions and dilutions contain o.2 per cent or less of morphine.
Where the figure entered in this column includes pure morphine contained in crude morphine, the
weight of such pure morphine and also the weight of the crude morphine should be indicated separately
under "Remarks".

 found on the market contain about go per cent of pure diacetylmorphine.
Cocabne: Hydrochloride of cocaine contains about go per cent of pure cocaine.
Nitrate of cocaine contains 75 per cent of pure cocaine.

of pure dihydrohydroxycodeinone.

codeinone.

dihydromorphinone.

acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine (acedicone) contains 9o per tent of pure acetyldihydrocodeinone.

morphine.
Benzylmorphine: Hydrochloride of benzylmorphine (peronine) contains 87 per cent of pure benzyl-
morphine.
Methylmorphine (codeine): Phosphate of codeine contains on an average 7o per cent of pure methyl-
morphine (codeine).
Hydrochloride of codeine contains 8r per cent of pure methylmorphine (codeine).
Sulphate of codeine contains 76 per cent of pure methylmorphine (codeine).
Ethylmorphine: Hydrochloride of ethylmorphine (dionine) contains 81 per cent of pure ethylmorphine.

UNITED NATIONS NATIONS UNIES
Lake Success, New York
EXECUTIVE OFFICE OF THE SECRETARY-GENERAL
Reference:
14 Apr11 1947
606-27-2/BA
The Secretary-General of the United Nations presents
his compliments to
and has the honour to draw attention to the decisions of the
Commission on Narcotic Drugs at its first session and to the
resolution of the Economic and Social Council at its fourth
session concerning the 1ssue of a questionnaire on the Limitation
and Control of the Cultivation of the Opium Poppy and the Pro-
duction of Raw Opium and the Control of Other Raw Materials used
in the Manufacture of Opium Alkaloids.
The Commission on Narcotic Drugs decided to issue the
questionnaire subject to the approval of the Economic and Social
Council which on 28 March 1947, during its fourth session,
adopted the following resolution:
"THE ECONOMIC AND SOCIAL COUNCIL
HAVING NOTED the importance of bringing a speedy solution
to the urgent problem of the limitation of production of
raw materials from which narcotic drugs are manufactured,
HAVING NOTED the preparatory work 1nitiated by the Commis-
sion with a view to holding an international conference
to deal with this problem
1. APPROVES the issue of the questionnaire on raw opium
prepared by the Commission on Narcotic Drugs (Document
E/251 Add.2) and requests the Secretary-General to trans-
mit this questionnaire to the governments concerned
asking them to communicate, on or before 15 August 1947,

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.99)
4COPY
-2-
the information called for therein and any observations
bearing on the subject which they may wish to submit,
The questionnaire issued in accordance with this resolution
(document E/CN.7/63) and enclosed herein, 1s therefore addressed
to all governments which are parties to the international con-
ventions relating to narcotic drugs, except Spain. The Secretary-
General has the honour to request that all governments of countries,
which during any year of the period covered by the questionnaire,
i.e. 1937-1946, e1ther cultivated the opium poppy for the produe-
tion of opium, or used poppy straw for the manufacture of morphine
and other opium alkaloids, would be good enough to comply with
the resolution of the Council and provide the information requested
in the questionnaire by 15 August 1947.
The Secretary-General has the honour also to request that
the governments which do not consider that their countries are
within any of the two categories mentioned above, should inform
him by 15 August 1947 that they are of the opinion that the
enclosed questionnaire is not applicable to them.

Convention for limiting the Manufacture and regulating the Distribution
of Narcotic Drugs of July 13th, 1931
PERMANENT CENTRAL OPIUM BOARD
ANNUAL STATISTICS OF IMPORTS AND EXPORTS OF
METHYLMORPHINE AND ETHYLMORPHINE
(To be forwarded to the Central Boand not t than March 31st.)
GOVERNMENT OF
19
COMPETENT DEPARTMENT
C/a
(Signed):
, Head of Department
These statisties relate to the calendar year 19
GENERAL INSTRUCTIONS
Show weight in kilogrammes and grammes, if impossible, state cle arly the weight used in the table
Fill in every column. Where there is nothing to report, write the word " nil".
Only net weights should be entered in the table (ie, excluding packing material, such as cases,
bottles, tubes and other containers, wrappers, etc).
Only the weight of the pure alkaloid content should be given in the case of crude alkaloids and of
salts. (See table of equivalences at the end of this form.)
REFERENCES
Article 13, paragraph 2, of the Convention of July 13th, 1931.
For the definitions, see Article I of that Convention.
NO1E
STATISTICS SHOULD BE BASED ON ACTUAL MOVEMENTS ACROSS THE FRONTIER
AND NOT MERELY UPON THE IMPORT AND EXPORT AUTHORISATIONS WHICH HAVE
BEEN ISSUED.
"Imported from ": Give the country which exported the goods and whose competent authorities
have issued the export authorisation according to Article 13 of the Geneva Convention of February 19th,
1925, but, if no such authorisation has been issued, the exporting country is the country from which
the goods were actually despatched to the importing country.
"Exported to ": Give the country which imported the goods and whose competent authorities
have issued the import certificate according to Articie 13, paragraph 2, of the Geneva Convention; but,
if no such certificate has been issued, the importing country is the country to which the goods were
actually despatched.
Import and export include consignments arriving or leaving by post.
Import is also intended to include entrance from abroad into a bonded warehouse, free port or free
zone, and export is also intended to include despatch abroad from a bonded warehouse, free port or free
zone, although such traffic may not ordinarily be treated by the national Customs laws as technical
import and export; but care should be taken to ensure that goods passing the Customs from a bonded
warehouse, free port or free zone into the country itself shall not be treated as imports, and goods trans-
ferred from the country itself into a bonded warehouse, free port or free zone situated in the country
shall not be treated as exports.
However, if a shipment passes in transit through the country to another country and is accompanied
by a proper export authorisation or diversion certificate (see Article 15 of the Geneva Convention of
February 19th, 1925), the country through which it passes in transit should not consider it as an
import and export, even if the shipment is placed for a time in a bonded warehouse, free port or free zone.
Goods returned by a country for any reason whatever to the original exporting country shall be
entered as exports by the one country and as imports by the other.

it if a Party to only one Convention could see its way to supplying the information required under the
other Convention also.

TABLE OF EQUIVALENCES
(By pure alkaloid is meant basic anhydrous alkaloid.)
Morphone: The principal morphine salts found on the market contain about 8o per cent of pure morphine.
Diacetylmorphine (Diamorphine, Heroin): The principal diacetylmorphine salts (diamorphine, heroin)
found on the market contain about 9o per cent of pure diacetylmorphine.
Cocaine: Hydrochloride of cocaine contains about go per cent of pure cocaine.
Nitrate of cocaine contains 75 per cent of pure cocaine.
Tincture of coca ordinarily contains o.2 per cent of pure cocaine.
Fluid extract of coca ordinarily contains o.6 per cent of pure cocaine.
Dihydrohydroxycodeinone: Hydrochloride of dihydrohydroxycodeinone (eucodal) contains 78 per cent
of pure dihydrohydroxycodeinone.
Dihydrocodeinone: Bitartrate of dihydrocodeinone (dicodide) contains 6o per cent of pure dihydro-
codeinone.
Dihydromorphinone: Hydrochloride of dihydromorphinone (dilaudide) contains 89 per cent of pure
dihydromorphinone.
Acetyldihydrocodeinone or acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine: Hydrochloride of acetyldihydrocodeinone or
acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine (acedicone) contains go per cent of pure acetyldihydrocodeinone.
Dihydromorphine: Hydrochloride of dihydromorphine (paramorfan) contains 89 per cent of pure dihydro-
morphine.
Benzylmorphine: Hydrochloride of benzylmorphine (peronine) contains 87 per cent of pure benzyl-
morphine.
Methylmorphine (codeine): Phosphate of codeine contains on an average 7o per cent of pure methyl-
morphine (codeine).
Hydrochloride of codeine contains 81 per cent of pure methylmorphine (codeine).
Sulphate of codeine contains 76 per cent of pure methylmorphine (codeine).
Ethylmorphine: Hydrochloride of ethylmorphine (dionine) contains 81 per cent of pure ethylmorphine.

REMARKS
Article 6. paragraph 2, of the Limitation Convention lays down that if, at the end of any year, any
High Contracting Party tinds that the amount manufactured exceeds the total of the amounts specified
in Article 6 (1) (a) to (e), less any deductions made under Article 7, paragraph 1, such excess shall be
deducted from the amount to be manufactured during the following year, and the reasons for any such
excess shall be stated in forwarding the annual statistics to the Permanent Central Board.
Please give, should necessity arise, in the space provided below, the amounts of any such excesses
and the reasons for them

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
NOTES
Note I. Morphine: This heading refers to morphine in the following forms: (a) pure morphine
(b) crude morphine; (c) salts of morphine; (d) preparations which contain more than 2o per cent of
morphine made direct from raw or medicinal opium; (e) preparations which contain more than o.2 per
cent of morphine made from any of the forms of morphine mentioned in (a), (b), (c) or (d); (f) solutiona
and dilutions of morphine in an inert substance, liquid or solid, made from any of the forms of morphine
mentioned in (a), (b), (c) or (d), even if these solutions and dilutions contain o.2 per cent or less of morphine.
Where the figure entered in this column includes pure morphine contained in crude morphine, the
weight of such pure morphine and also the weight of the crude morphine should be indicated separately
under " Remarks"
Note 2. Cocaine: Thus heading refers to cocaine in the following forms: (a) pure cocaine; (b) salts
of cocaine: (c) preparations which contain more than o.1 per cent of cocaine made direct from the coca
leaf; (d) preparations which contain more than o.1 per cent of cocaine made from any of the forms of
cocaine mentioned in (a), (b) or (c); (e) solutions and dilutions of cocaine in an inert substance, liquid
or solid, made from any of the forms of cocaine mentioned in (a), (b) or (c), even if these solutions and
dilutions contain o.1 per cent or less of cocaine.
Note 3. When figures are given for the substances mentioned below in answer to the questions
in the present form, the quantities for each drug should be inserted in the blank columns, additional
columns being added, if necessary:
Dihydromorphine and its salts (paramorfan) and preparations;
Morphine-N-oxide (genomorphine) and its preparations;
Thebaine and its salts and preparations;
Ecgonine and its salts and preparations;
The esters of ecgonine and their salts and preparations;
The esters of morphine — except diacetylmorphine — and their salts and preparations;
Benzylmorphine and its salts (peronine) and preparations;
The other ethers of morphine and their salts and preparations, except methylmorphine (codeine)
and its salts and preparations and ethylmorphine and its salts (dionine) and preparations;
The esters of the following: dihydrohydroxycodeinone, dihydrocodeinone, dihydromorphinone,
acctyldihydrocodeinone or acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine, dihydromorphine and their
salts and preparations;
 The morphine-N-oxide derivatives and the other pentavalent nitrogen morphine derivatives
and their preparations.

GENERAL INSTRUCTIONS
Show weight in kilogramines and grammes; if impossible, state cle arly the weight used in the table.
Fill in every column. Where there is nothing to report, write the word " nil".
Only net weights should be entered in the table (i.e., excluding packing material, such as cases,
bottles, tubes and other containers, wrappers, etc.).
Only the weight of the pure alkaloid content should be given in the case of crude alkaloids and of
salts. (See table of equivalences at the end of this form.)
REFERENCES
Article 13, paragraph 2, of the Convention of July 13th, 1931.
For the definitions, see Article I of that Convention.
NOIE
STATISTICS SHOULD BE BASED ON ACTUAL MOVEMENTS ACROSS THE FRONTIER
AND NOT MERELY UPON THE IMPORT AND EXPORT AUTHORISATIONS WHICH HAVE
BEEN ISSUED.
"Imported from ": Give the country which exported the goods and whose competent authorities
have issued the export authorisation according to Article 13 of the Geneva Convention of February 19th,
1925, but, if no such authorisation has been issued, the exporting country is the country from which
the goods were actually despatched to the importing country.
"Exported to": Give the country which imported the goods and whose competent authorities
have issued the import certificate according to Article 13, paragraph 2, of the Geneva Convention; but,
if no such certificate has been issued, the importing country is the country to which the goods were
actually despatched.
Import and export include consignments arriving or leaving by post.
Import is also intended to include entrance from abroad into a bonded warehouse, free port or free
zone, and export is also intended to inclu de despatch abroad from a bonded warehouse, free port or free
zone, although such traffic may not ordinarily be treated by the national Customs laws as technical
import and export; but care should be taken to ensure that goods passing the Customs from a bonded
warehouse, free port or free zone into the country itself shall not be treated as imports, and goods trans-
ferred from the country itself into a bonded warehouse, free port or free zone situated in the country
shall not be treated as exports.
However, if a shipment passes in transit through the country to another country and is accompanied
by a proper export authorisation or diversion certificate (see Article 15 of the Geneva Convention of
February r9th, 1925), the country through which it passes in transit should not consider it as an
import and export, even if the shipment is placed for a time in a bonded warehouse, free port or free zone.
Goods returned by a country for any reason whatever to the original exporting country shall be
entered as exports by the one country and as imports by the other.

Note 4. By imports " for Government purposes  is to be understood substances imported for the
use of the military, naval and air forces of the country, or to meet exceptional circumstances. Substances
imported by the Government for normal domestic consumption by the civilian population, whether
the substances are to be sold or to be distributed gratuitously by the Government, should not be con-
sidered as imports for Government purposes.
Nol 5. Statistics should be based on actual movements across the frontier and not merely
upon the import and export authorisations which have been issued.
"Imported from": Give the country which exported the goods and whose competent authorities
have issued the export authorisation according to Article 13 ot the Geneva Convention; but, if no such
authorisation has been issued, the exporting country is the country from which the goods were actually
despatched to the importing country.
"Exported to ": Give the country which imported the goods and whose competent authorities
have issued the import certificate according to Article 1. paragraph 2. of the Geneva Convention;
but, if no such certiticate has been issued. the importing country is the country to which the goods were
actually despatched.
Inmiport and export include consignments arriving or leaving by post.
Import is also intended to include entrance from abroad into a bonded warehouse, free port or free
zone, and export is also intended to inchude despatch abroad from a bonded warehouse, free port or free
zone, although such traffic may not ordinarily be treated by the national Customs laws as technical
inport and export; but care should be taken to ensure that goods passing the Customs from a bonded
warehouse, free port or free zone into the country itself shall not be treated as imports, and goods trans-
ferred from the country itself into a bonded warehouse. fiee port or free zone situated in the country
shall not be treated as exports.
However, if a shipment passes in transit through the country to another country and is accompanied
by a proper export authorisation or diversion certificate (see Article 15 of the Geneva Convention), the
country through which it passes in transit should not consider it as an import and export, even if the
shipment is placed for a time in a bonded warehouse, free port or free zone.
Goods returned by a country for any reason whatever to the original exporting country shall be
entered as exports by the one country and as imports by the other.

Article 3. The word "Narcotic Dealer" as used in this Regulation shall include
a person who may be lawfull: entitled to compow d, produce, sell, deal in,
deliver, dispense or otherwige distribute nercotia or narcotic drugs.
In accordanee with the kind of activitles, arcotie dealers shall be
classified into narcotic ccmpounder or preducer, nre tic central wholesele
dealer, narcotic local whclesale dealer, nercotic Ietail dealer, narcotic
practitioner, dealer in exempt narcotic preparation3, and narcotic research
worker. A narcotic cempounder is a person, who by compounding or mixing,
produces narcotic drugs or preperations for sale or distribution in original
sealed packages as provided for in this Rcgulation.
A narcotic producer is a person who produces na cotic drugs or
preparations to be sold not by mixing or compounding, but merely
transferring the contents of one package or of a aumber of packages
to one or more packages of the same or of greater or cmaller size.
A narcotic wholesale dealer (including central and local) is a person
who sells or offers for sale narcotic drugs or preparations in original
sealed packages.
A narcotic retail dealer is a person who sells narcotic drugs or
preparations from original sealed package with or without compounding,
pursuant to prescriptions written by registered narcotic practitioners in
the course of professional practice.

APO 235 UNIT 3
Report by the Government of Korea for the Calendar Year 1946
on the Traffic in Opium and other Dangerous Drugs
A- GENERAL
I. Laws and Publications.
1. Ordinance No. 119, Narcotics Control, dated 11 November 1946.
2. A law to control all aources of-and activities connected with
narcotic drugs in Korea, south of 38° north latitude, and to limit the
supply and usee thereof to legitimate medical and scientific require-
ments. (Copy enclosed.)
3. None.
II. Administration.
1. (a) All phases of narcotic drug control were transferred from
the Monopoly Bureau, Finance Department, to Bureau of Pharmaceutical.
Affaire of the Department of Public Health and Welfare, Governnent of
.Korea.
(b) Not applicable.
(c) Insufficient activity to warrant an accurate statement.
2. The Korean populetion is relatively free of addiction; but ap-
parently repatriates to Korea are highly addicted. Amone the Korean
Nationals coming tack from Manchria, China, Formosa and Japan, are
many addicts and persons engaging in illicit traffic of narcotice.
The number of addicts is now believed to be higher than at any time in
the history of Korea.
3. Fork is continuing toward thie end.
.III. Control of International Trede.
1. No inports or exporte have been made, although South Korea has
been supplied with narcotic drugs from surplus United States Army stocks.

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.98)
2. Authority to issue import certificates and export auti orizn
tions wes transferred frou the Konopoly Bureau to the Hareotis cee.
of Pharueceutical Affairs Bureau alone with other ectivities of the
arcotic Section.
3. Regulations for control of inport certificates and export
authorizatione ere being formulated to comply with all existing re
quiremente of international conventions for handling of dangerouc drugs.
4. There were no narcotic drug exports from Koree in 1946.
5. No trensactions.
6. No transections.
7. No traneactions.
9. No Indian hemp has been imported in 1946.
IV. International Cooperation.
1. None have been entered into.
2. No trade with any country has been entered into thie year.
3. Regulations pertaining to import and export of narcotic drues
are being formulated at the present time and they will comply with all
international conventions relating to the control of narcotic drugs.
v. Illicit Traffic.
1. The illicit traffic of narcotic drugs in Korea during the past
year has been primerily from former Japanese stocks, both army and
civilian; and secondly, from local clandestine production. No distri-
bution of narcotics was made during 1946 and all transactions were
ordered stopped in 1945 shortly after arrival of American forces,pending
promulgation of laws and regulations. Hospitals, clinics and practi-
tioners continued to use their stocks on hand. until exhausted. Very
smell amounts of narcotics from outside Korea were evident during 1946.
It is well established that there were large amounts of narcotics di-
verted from government control just prior to the arrival of American
troops. Some of this was recovered, but much is still in stock of
pharmacies, hospitals, and practitioners. This along with the normal
stocks formerly carried has been sufficient to meet medical needs
until recently. Opium poppy eroving was encouraged and exploited
under the Japanese and despite prohibitions to the contrary and as wide-
spread dissemination of information on the prohibition as possible, it
is believed that considerable opium was srown and harvested in the
more remote and mountainous areas of South Korea. This has been the
main source of supply for addicts.

Japanese.

flscated from chennels for addicte were very highly adulterated and of

known that considerable opium was produced; however, there is no way
to estirate how much it may have been, or whet disposition wae made of
it. The most of it was perhaps coneumed in South Korea.
The miesion of Americen forces at that time was diser ing
